## Grade 9 (ມ.4) Student Textbook — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-10-27

The fourth book of the SECONDARY series ("Book Four") — it follows
Book Three (Grade 8, filed above) in the same seven-book run for
the 12-year school system, and it closes the Somphone-and-Jennifer
story line the secondary books have carried since Book One: in
Unit 10 Jennifer's family leaves Laos for Australia. Source copy:
the public mirror the library's secured copy came from (GitHub
Xaypanya/Lao-Textbook, `textbooks/secondary/m4/m4_english.pdf`).
Working copy verified against the library record before reading:
**11,527,173 bytes, 152 PDF pages — both match the record exactly
(VERIFIED-TEXT in the record; the English student entry)**.
**Content verified by cover, not by filename alone** (the Grade 7
guide lesson): PDF p.1 rendered is this book's own combined
cover-and-credits page — "ENGLISH FOR SECONDARY SCHOOLS," the
series mascot holding a big pink **"4,"** Ministry of Education
**and Sports** / Research Institute for Educational Sciences,
**2013** — and PDF p.2 is its Introduction, opening "English
textbook for secondary school Book Four is the fourth in a series
of seven textbooks." The file is the book its record says it is.
Read page by page from the text layer; the two picture-carried
pages that matter (the food-groups wheel, printed p.2, and
Jennifer's torn-up letter, printed p.126) were read on renders;
the Lao layer extracts as mojibake under the series' legacy font
encoding, and the English layer is doubled (a ghost layer repeats
most lines) — both quirks are the file's, not the print's.

## The book itself

"ENGLISH FOR SECONDARY SCHOOLS" — no grade number in the title;
the cover badge is the pink **"4"** and the record's Grade 9
(ມ.4, secondary year 4) placement is the series position (Book
Four of seven). Unlike Book Three, which spread cover and credits
over two pages, Book Four prints its credits **on the cover
itself** (the Book Two way): **the same credits team as Books
One–Three, name for name** — Writers Manivone Phaxayavong,
Bouakeo Vilaylath, Thongsouk Keomany, Kittisack Saychoummany,
Bounthapany Bounxouay and Khambane Pasanchay; Managing Editors
Khamphanh Phimsipasom and Viengkham Phonpraseuth; Editor in
Chief Phouangkham Somsanith, Assoc. Prof. Dr. Bouasavanh
Keovilay; Design and Layout Khankab Phongoudom. (Book Three's
extra "in editing collaboration" line does not appear.) Cover
imprint year **2013** (Book One 2010, Book Two 2011, Book Three
2012). The Introduction (PDF p.2, printed p. I) states the book's
place: the fourth of seven textbooks answering the reform of
general education from 11 years to 12; the extra secondary year
was added in academic year 2009–2010; written by the Foreign
Language Resource and Research Centre at RIES with the Faculty
of Letters, National University of Laos, funded by the **Basic
Education Sector Development Project (BESDP)**; the same
admission as the earlier books ("the textbook is produced under
the time pressure; therefore, some minor errors could not be
avoided"). The Lao half of the Introduction names RIES in full.
Contents (PDF pp.3–4, printed pp. II–III): **11 units — nine
content units of three lessons each (27 lessons), with a
REVISION unit after Unit 4 (Unit 5) and a closing REVISION unit
(Unit 11)** — where Book Three ran eleven straight content units
with no revisions at all, Book Four brings the revision units
back — and **References** listed at p.147. In the book itself the
back matter is a **Glossaries** (English–Lao word list, printed
pp.147–148) closing with a **Bibliography** (printed p.148):
Gammidge's Grammar Works 1–2 (CUP 1998), Vale's Picture Grammar
for Children 1, Seidl's Grammar One (OUP 1998), New Headway
Beginner (2006), New English File Elementary (2006, its year
given here — Book Three's References left it blank), Side by
Side Activity Workbook 1 (2001), and the series' own ancestor,
"English for Lao Secondary Schools book 3" (Phandamnong et al.,
1998). Printed page = PDF page − 4 through the body (front matter
carries Roman folios I–III on PDF pp.2–4); the book ends at
printed p.148 — **there is no blank back-cover panel**; all 152
PDF pages carry print. **No ISBN is printed anywhere** — checked
on the cover/credits page (render) and across the whole text
layer, which contains no ISBN string. PDF metadata: title "TB_M4
Eng cover.psd," author RIES, Acrobat 8.1 Combine Files (2012).

The book's teaching shape is the series' fixed one — a
vocabulary box with Lao glosses, Listen and repeat, then numbered
activities — but two shapes now stand as regular features:
**"Read and translate"** (a full text read and translated into
Lao, in at least six lessons) and the boxed **Grammar note**
mid-lesson. Writing climbs again: two-paragraph tasks arrive in
Unit 2, process writing in Unit 3, and personal-letter writing
closes the book's story in Unit 10.

## The map, unit by unit

- **Unit 1 THE FOOD (pp.1–16).** *Lesson 1 The food we need
  (pp.1–5):* advice with **should / shouldn't** over a health
  frame. The reading text (pp.1–2, second half picture-carried
  beside a five-food-groups wheel, read on the render) sorts
  food into **five groups — proteins, carbohydrates, vitamins,
  minerals, fats and oils** — with where each is found; then
  read-and-list sorting, five written questions, listen-and-say
  true/false, an 12-gap listening cloze of the same text, and a
  doctor-and-patient dialogue ("What should I do? — You should
  eat healthy food… You shouldn't eat a lot of fats and pickled
  food," p.5); spelling-contest game. *Lesson 2 Like and dislike
  (pp.6–10):* like/dislike sentences, **Would you like…? — Yes,
  please / No, thanks** set against Do you like…?, a restaurant
  dialogue (Somphone asks Jennifer what she thinks of Lao food —
  she likes sticky rice and grilled chicken, dislikes papaya
  salad), a tick/cross survey of Jennifer's tastes, a **class
  survey** of three classmates, an oral report shape ("name 1
  likes fried rice, cake and lobster but he/she dislikes grilled
  chicken and pork"), and the chant **"Food I like to eat"**
  printed in full (p.10). *Lesson 3 Offering advice (pp.11–16):*
  the **present simple** taught whole — verb-choice exercises,
  a boxed Grammar note (Subject + V1(s) + Object, eat/eats,
  p.12), the read-and-translate text "Daravone's teeth"
  (Somphone's sister who has never had toothache), a How often…?
  class survey — and three closing Grammar notes: **should /
  shouldn't** as a table (p.15), **like vs would like** (p.16),
  and **adverbs of frequency on a 100%→0% scale** (always,
  usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never; once/twice/three
  times/every… at the end of the sentence, p.16).
- **Unit 2 TRAVELLING (pp.17–28).** *Lesson 1 Tourist sites
  (pp.17–21):* advice about where to visit with **should and
  ought to** — a Grammar note (Should + V1 / Ought to + V1,
  p.17), the dialogue "Somphone's advice" (Khonephapheng, Wat
  Phou, the Plain of Jars, Luang Prabang; "you ought to go to
  Luang Prabang"), true/false on the dialogue, picture questions
  for four regions, should/ought to dialogue completion, and the
  book's **first two-paragraph writing task** (p.20–21): one
  paragraph about a place you have visited (starter given), one
  about a place you should/ought to visit. *Lesson 2 The old
  capital (pp.22–25):* describing places. The read-and-translate
  text **Luang Prabang** (the old capital, a World Heritage site
  "situated in the north and surrounded by mountains," its
  temples, Ban Phanom weaving village, festivals, food) — the
  passive shapes sit inside the description unlabelled here —
  then a listening cloze of the same text, underline-the-
  adjectives, describing a traditional stilt house against a
  modern concrete one from sentence starters, and **write two
  paragraphs describing the place or city where you live**
  (p.25). *Lesson 3 My best holiday (pp.26–28):* past simple
  and present perfect revised together in a holiday dialogue
  (Jennifer's family flew to Luang Prabang, as Somphone advised
  in Lesson 1), past-tense question practice, and the frame
  **superlative + present perfect: "This is the best holiday
  I've ever had"** with its set (nicest present, most wonderful
  old city, most delicious food, most beautiful place, p.27);
  **write a paragraph about the best holiday you've ever had**
  (p.28).
- **Unit 3 HOW IS IT MADE? (pp.29–42) — the passive unit.**
  *Lesson 1 Lao weaving (pp.29–32):* the lesson head states the
  aim outright — "using **passive verbs** and some logical
  connectors: **firstly, secondly, thirdly, finally**." The
  read-and-translate process text **Weaving** runs the steps in
  passives (tools are collected and prepared, patterns are
  chosen, threads are dyed, threads are placed in the frame,
  the fabric is cut); the sentences are re-practised, answered
  on, and cloze-filled; then the book's first passive Grammar
  note: **Present passive voice — Subject + to be + past
  participle (V3)** with a worked table (The classroom is
  cleaned every day / Vegetables are sold at the market / Plum
  pudding is eaten at Christmas, p.32), a fill-in exercise, and
  a V1–V2–V3 list. *Lesson 2 Rice growing (pp.33–37):* farming
  vocabulary over ten pictures; the read-and-translate text
  **How rice is grown** — present passives for the yearly cycle
  (land is ploughed and harrowed, seeds are soaked, seedlings
  are pulled up and replanted, rice is cut, collected and stored
  in a barn) with past passives for the old way ("rice was
  normally grown once a year"); a picture-ordering exercise,
  true/false **with the correct sentence written out**, a
  **write-the-process task** (how vegetables, flowers or plants
  are grown, from a six-step diagram: choose plants → prepare
  the soil → put the plants into the soil → cover the roots →
  water → …, connectors printed as helps), and the song
  **Farmer Bounta** (the Old MacDonald tune with a Lao farmer,
  five verses printed in full, p.37). *Lesson 3 Things found
  (pp.38–42):* the **present perfect passive** — Grammar note
  (Subject + have/has + been + V3: My shoes have been found /
  The TV has been fixed, p.38), Have they been found? /
  Has it been fixed yet? dialogues, the two-part lost-shoes
  dialogue (Jennifer's shoes taken by mistake; "Your shoes have
  been found"), and a write-and-say set that builds a lost-item
  sentence pair for nine picture prompts.
- **Unit 4 A PICNIC (pp.43–58).** *Lesson 1 Planning an outing
  (pp.43–48):* **will** revised whole (full will/'ll/won't
  contraction table, p.43) and the book's conditional: a Grammar
  note headed **"Present Real Condition"** — If + subject + V1…,
  subject + will + V1…, in both orders ("If it rains, we'll go
  by bus," p.44) — sentence writing, a choose-the-correct-verb
  exercise that drills the if-clause present against will
  ("If Somphone has/will have time, he'll go to the zoo"),
  a zoo-trip dialogue where every plan is conditional on the
  weather, picture-cued if-sentences, and word-order building.
  *Lesson 2 The zoo (pp.49–53):* twelve zoo animals matched to
  pictures; the read-and-translate text **The zoo**; a zoo-keeper
  dialogue dense with first conditionals ("The cage will be
  finished next week if it doesn't rain… If the panda arrives
  soon, there won't be enough"); an exercise that picks every
  conditional sentence out of the dialogue; sentence completion;
  and a ten-clue logic puzzle over six cages (animal, country,
  feeding time). *Lesson 3 Asking for permission (pp.54–58):*
  formal and informal permission as one system — **Is it OK if
  I…? / Can I…? / Could I… please?** with Yes, sure / Yes,
  certainly / Sorry, I'm afraid you can't — two dialogues
  (Jennifer asks her father, then her teacher Miss Smith, for
  Friday off), a substitution table, a **letter cloze** (Jennifer
  to her grandmother about the zoo and Nam Ngum Dam trip, 15
  gaps), a formal-or-informal sorting exercise, and **write a
  letter like it about your last holiday**.
- **Unit 5 REVISION (pp.59–65)** — Units 1–4 revised. Grammar:
  an irregular-verb three-form table to complete; **for or
  since**; **write about things your grandparents used to do**
  (used to in production); a listening cloze that rebuilds an
  accident text in past simple against past continuous ("while
  I was going to school, I saw an accident"); advice-writing
  with should/shouldn't for seven problems; a listening cloze
  that re-runs **How rice is grown** with every passive blanked;
  conditional completion; and **error-correction of the present
  perfect passive** ("My computer has not been fix yet / They
  have been find / They haven't been bring back yet" — printed
  as the errors to fix, p.63). Vocabulary: describing-people
  listening cloze, made-of sentences from a materials box,
  What's the matter? picture answers. Dialogue: a describing-a-
  friend cloze and invitation/offer matching.
- **Unit 6 PEOPLE FROM THE PAST (pp.66–84).** *Lesson 1 Prince
  Souphanouvong — part 1 (pp.66–72):* history vocabulary
  (prince, province, civil engineering, the Lao Patriotic
  Movement, ministries) and the book's fullest **past simple
  Grammar note**: regular verbs tabled (study→studied …
  shop→shopped) with affirmative/negative frames (pp.67–68),
  then **irregular verbs** tabled (sleep→slept, read→read,
  go→went …, p.69) with completion practice; the biography
  part 1 as read-and-translate (born July 13, 1909 at Ban Mai
  village, Luang Prabang; Hanoi, then Paris and the National
  School of Bridge and Road Engineering, graduated 1937;
  projects across Indochina; joined the Lao Patriotic Movement
  in 1940 — the text runs past the page foot; summary only
  here), written questions, cue-built sentences, picture talk,
  **write about somebody you know well** (past-tense life
  writing from a model), Hangman. *Lesson 2 Prince
  Souphanouvong — part 2 (pp.73–79):* **wh-questions about the
  past** — Yes/No-question dialogue practice over a 16-item
  last-weekend chart, a wh-question interview chart, and a
  **question-forms table** (Wh- + did + subject + infinitive +
  time expression, p.75); biography part 2 as read-and-translate
  (Minister of Planning 1957–59, Vice Prime Minister 1962,
  elected President of the Lao PDR in 1975, served until 1991,
  died of a heart attack on January 9, 1995 — summary only),
  true/false with corrections, written questions, a
  read-and-choose that runs the biography's own sentences
  against wrong forms (born/was born, elected/was elected),
  and a word-box cloze of part 2. *Lesson 3 Ho Chi Minh
  (pp.80–84):* past simple revised; a Grammar note of **past
  time expressions** (yesterday, last night/week/year, in 2009,
  two years ago, p.80) with ordering and word-order practice
  and a wh-question completion set; a six-sentence picture
  match; the biography as read-and-translate (a Vietnamese
  politician, poet and journalist; born May 19, 1890 in Kiem
  Lien [as printed]; Hue and Saigon; work on ships, photography
  in Paris; founder of the Democratic Republic of Vietnam; died
  September 3, 1969 in Hanoi [as printed] — summary only);
  written questions; a **listen-and-correct-the-wrong-
  information** version of the same text; and an anagram set
  (coalition, assembly, revolutionary, independence, minister,
  movement, patriotic, liberate, politician, founder, freedom).
- **Unit 7 PEOPLE IN OUR LIVES (pp.85–98).** *Lesson 1 My best
  friend (pp.85–89):* describing people — build and hair
  vocabulary matched over ten pictured children; a Grammar-note
  table **asking and giving people's general description**
  (What does he look like? / Does he wear glasses? / How old is
  he? — He's in his twenties / What colour is her hair? / How
  tall is she? — She's 152 cm, p.86); write-the-question
  practice; an **is-or-has exercise** for the 's form that
  quietly re-uses the perfect and the perfect passive ("Jennifer's
  got curly hair — has; My bicycle's been found — has," p.87);
  a who-is-she dialogue that identifies Jennifer from her
  description; a gapped "My best friend" text about Jennifer;
  and two writing tasks — **describe a classmate for the class
  to guess**, and **describe somebody in your family** (p.89).
  *Lesson 2 My photo album (pp.90–93):* more description —
  a photo-album dialogue ("It was taken a long time ago, when I
  was in Savannakhet… He looks like a boxer. He's got broad
  shoulders and very strong arms"), adjective scales to extend
  (height short→tall, age young→elderly, looks, hair, p.91),
  a hotel dialogue, eight person descriptions to match to
  pictures (numbering as printed: 1, 2, 2, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8),
  a complete-the-table grid, and an adjective word-search.
  *Lesson 3 A kind and generous person (pp.94–98):* personality
  — a paired adjective set (talkative, patient/impatient,
  friendly/unfriendly, helpful/unhelpful, kind/unkind, shy,
  nervous, calm, generous, clever, a good sense of humour);
  describe-yourself models; a boyfriend dialogue ("What kind of
  person is he?"); true/false; a ten-question **personality
  questionnaire** with three printed result types (Type A quiet
  and careful, Type B careful and confident, Type C confident
  and outgoing, pp.96–97); name-your-classmates adjective
  matching with group comparison; and adjective-to-definition
  matching.
- **Unit 8 FEELINGS (pp.99–111).** *Lesson 1 I'm upset!
  (pp.99–102):* the feelings set grows up — **bored, lonely,
  tired, angry, upset, disappointed, frightened** matched
  English-to-Lao and used in sentences; a face-picture set
  (frightened, worried, happy, angry, surprised, bored, sad,
  ill) with **How does Sonphet feel? — He feels surprised**;
  situation cards answered from a second set (hungry, sick,
  thirsty, happy, sad, terrible, tired); two What's the matter?
  dialogues (a noisy neighbour's party; a terrible haircut);
  and **write five sentences about yourself** with the feelings
  words. *Lesson 2 Hobbies (pp.103–107):* a 22-hobby list
  sorted indoors/outdoors/both; the read-and-translate text
  **Bouaphanh's hobbies** (keen on football, reading to relax,
  cooking, singing — summary only); true/false with corrections;
  written questions; -ing completions; a six-person hobby-table
  paragraph puzzle; word-order writing; and the lesson's
  Grammar note: **like / enjoy + noun, like / enjoy + verb-ing,
  be keen on + noun or verb-ing, be fond of + noun** ("We
  usually use fond of for people or animals," p.107). *Lesson 3
  Interests (pp.108–111):* musical instruments named and
  matched; **be interested in** — a concert dialogue ("Are you
  interested in music? — Yes, I am. I'm interested in all kinds
  of music"), a five-student group interview over nine interests
  (Lao music, pop music, geography, history, languages, arts,
  sports, computers), and **write the sentences about the four
  students** ("Student B is interested in Lao music, but she is
  not interested in sports"); spelling contest.
- **Unit 9 SCHOOL SPORTS (pp.112–123).** *Lesson 1 The sports
  carnival (pp.112–116):* nine sports matched; a timetable
  dialogue; a **listen-and-complete plan of events** (six days,
  sports, start/finish times, M1 vs M2-style team pairings) with
  seven written questions; choose-the-question-to-fit-the-answer;
  a nine-word sports word-search. *Lesson 2 An exciting football
  match (pp.117–120):* football vocabulary (referee, goalkeeper,
  line man, whistle, break the rules); a rules-explaining
  dialogue; the narrative text **An Exciting Football Match**
  (M2 vs M3, M2 scores first, M3 wins 3–1, "the most exciting
  football match of the year" — summary only) with written
  questions and true/false with corrections; a past-tense
  completion of the same match; and **write five football
  rules** from a must-not model. *Lesson 3 Congratulations!
  (pp.121–123):* congratulating as its own exchange — **Well
  done! / Wonderful! / Congratulations! / Excellent! / Great!**
  in six short dialogues (a medal, the highest mark, a baby, a
  driving test, a new job, 85% in English), picture practice,
  and a listen-and-complete built entirely of congratulations.
- **Unit 10 I'LL MISS LAOS (pp.124–137)** — the farewell unit.
  *Lesson 1 A personal letter (pp.124–129):* letter-writing
  taught from two real letters. **Nita's letter** (an English
  student at the National University of Laos writing to her
  sister Lida in Savannakhet, dated October 16, 2009 — summary
  only) with true/false-with-correction and write-the-questions
  exercises; then **Jennifer's letter to her friend Stella in
  Sydney, torn up** — seven numbered scraps to put in order
  (read on the render, printed p.126: the apology opening, the
  places she has been — Luang Prabang, Wat Phou and
  Khonephapheng, the Plain of Jars — "My time in Laos has gone
  so quickly," going home to continue her studies); its lines
  then sorted by tense, leading to the book's deepest present
  perfect Grammar note: **ever and never** (ever in questions,
  never in negatives), **just and yet** (just in positives, yet
  in negatives and questions), **been and gone** (been = went
  and came back; gone = on the way or there), p.127 — followed
  by tick-the-correct-sentence (perfect against past with
  yesterday), ever/yet/just conversation completion, and
  **write a letter to your friend** using the two letters as
  models. *Lesson 2 Jennifer's future (pp.130–134):* the future
  divided — a farewell-party dialogue, then a Grammar note:
  **be going to for plans we have decided on; will with maybe,
  probably, I guess, or I think for possible plans before a
  decision** (p.131); dialogue and conversation completions; the
  text **Leaving Vientiane** (the family will fly back Saturday;
  tidying, packing, the farewell party, dancing Lamvong —
  summary only) with true/false corrections; read-and-choose
  going to or will; a nine-blank completion; and guided future
  writing (answer five plan questions, then write about the
  trip). *Lesson 3 Goodbye and good luck! (pp.135–137):*
  farewells as their own lesson — two dialogues (the staff room:
  "Have a good trip home. We'll miss you"; the airport: "Thank
  you for coming to see us off… I'd like to wish you a safe
  journey and good luck… Take care of yourself"), three
  completion dialogues (bus station, airport, classroom —
  "Have a nice weekend"), a which-dialogue-is-a-long-goodbye
  question, and **write two dialogues**: a weekend goodbye and
  a going-abroad goodbye.
- **Unit 11 REVISION (pp.138–146)** — Units 5–10 revised, per
  its headnote (Units 6–10 in substance). Grammar: past tense
  (regular forms, irregular simple forms, a shopping dialogue,
  completed past dialogues, and a Ho Chi Minh word-box cloze);
  present perfect (choose-the-correct-response; a lost-watch
  conversation completed in past against perfect); **future
  with be going to and will** (a weekend-plans conversation,
  four going-to sentences, four will-with-maybe sentences, an
  Are you going to…? partner survey of ten activities).
  Vocabulary: word-to-Lao-meaning matching, translate-nine-
  phrases, sentence-order and opposite-adjective work, an
  appearance/personality sorting chart, feelings completion.
  Dialogues: write-the-response items, the farewell-party
  dialogue as a cloze, and the Unit 10 goodbye dialogues
  completed from word boxes — the revision names Jennifer's
  teacher **Miss Smith** where Unit 10's own staff-room dialogue
  names her Miss Buavone (Unit 4's permission lesson also used
  Miss Smith); recorded as printed.
- **Back matter (printed pp.147–148):** the **Glossaries** —
  an English–Lao word list (A–S visible across the two pages;
  entries include usage notes, e.g. "Fruit … (no s)"; "Sport —
  no s / Sports — used as adjective") — closing with the
  **Bibliography** above.

## What's new against Grade 8

Book Three taught the present perfect, used to, the past
continuous and must/have to, and never named the passive. Book
Four steps past it on four fronts:

- **The passive arrives as a named system** — one whole unit
  (Unit 3): the **present passive** tabled (p.32), the past
  passive inside the rice text, and the **present perfect
  passive** tabled (have/has + been + V3, p.38). Book Three's
  unlabelled shapes ("is made of," "is caused by") become
  Book Four's taught grammar.
- **Conditionals arrive, named**: the **first conditional** as
  the "Present Real Condition," tabled in both orders (p.44)
  and practised through a whole unit. Book Three had no
  conditional system at all. Only the first conditional appears
  — no second or third conditional anywhere in the book.
- **The present perfect continuous does NOT arrive** — stated
  plainly: the perfect is deepened (ever/never, just/yet,
  been/gone, p.127) but its continuous form is never taught in
  Book Four, so Laos still cannot stock Level 18's Present
  Perfect Continuous lesson.
- **The future is divided for the first time**: will is revised
  whole in Unit 4, then Unit 10 sets **be going to (decided
  plans) against will with maybe/probably/I think/I guess
  (undecided)** in one Grammar note (p.131) — Book Two's single
  going-to table grown into a two-way choice.
- Smaller arrivals: **ought to** beside should (p.17);
  **like/enjoy + V-ing, keen on, fond of, interested in** as
  tabled patterns (pp.107, 110); the **present simple re-tabled**
  with the frequency scale (pp.12, 16); the **past simple
  re-tabled whole** — regular and irregular lists (pp.67–69)
  where Book One only introduced it; **formal vs informal**
  named as a contrast (permission, p.58); personality language
  (Unit 7 Lesson 3) and a feelings set that runs to upset and
  frightened (Unit 8).
- Reading and writing step up again: biography as full
  read-and-translate texts (Souphanouvong in two parts, Ho Chi
  Minh), a match-report narrative, **listen-and-correct-the-
  wrong-information**, a **torn-up letter to re-order**, and
  writing that runs to two paragraphs (pp.20–21, 25), a process
  from a diagram (p.36), descriptions for a guessing game
  (p.89), and free personal letters (pp.58, 129). The Revision
  units return (Units 5 and 11) after Book Three dropped them.
  The Jennifer story ends: she flies home to Australia in
  Unit 10.

## New for the course (keyed to the built lessons)

- **Level 18, The Passive** — Laos can now stock it directly:
  the present passive Grammar note with its V3 list (U3 L1,
  p.32), process texts in passives (pp.30, 35), the present
  perfect passive tabled (U3 L3, p.38), error-correction of the
  perfect passive (U5, p.63). Laos goes in as a teaching
  country for the passive, not only for made-of shapes.
- **Level 18, Conditionals** — Laos stocks the first
  conditional as a named system, the "Present Real Condition"
  (U4 L1, p.44), drilled across the picnic unit (pp.45–53) and
  revised (U5, p.62).
- **Level 18, The Present Perfect** — Laos deepens it: the
  superlative + ever frame (U2 L3, p.27) and the book's fullest
  perfect Grammar note — ever/never, just/yet, been/gone
  (U10 L1, p.127). **The Present Perfect Continuous stays
  unstocked from Laos** — never taught in this book.
- **Level 12, Going To Later** — the two-way future note:
  going to for decided plans, will with maybe/probably/I
  think/I guess for undecided ones (U10 L2, p.131), plus will
  revised whole (U4 L1, p.43).
- **Level 12, Present Actions / Past Actions** — the present
  simple tabled with the 100%→0% frequency scale (U1 L3,
  pp.12, 16); the past simple tabled whole, regular and
  irregular (U6 L1, pp.67–69), past time expressions (U6 L3,
  p.80), and wh-questions about the past with their
  question-forms table (U6 L2, p.75).
- **Level 13, Describing Words in a Sentence** — the
  description table (What does he look like? … She's 152 cm,
  U7 L1, p.86), adjective scales (U7 L2, p.91), the personality
  set with its opposites (U7 L3, pp.94–98), and superlatives at
  work in the match report (U9 L2, pp.118–119).
- **Level 5, How Are You** — the feelings set grown up:
  upset, frightened, lonely, disappointed with I feel / I'm
  (U8 L1, pp.99–102), beside Grade 7's earlier set.
- **Level 5, Conversation** — permission formal and informal
  (Is it OK if I…? / Could I… please?, U4 L3, pp.54–58),
  congratulations as their own exchanges (U9 L3, p.121), and
  farewells whole — "Have a good trip home. We'll miss you,"
  "wish you a safe journey and good luck" (U10 L3, pp.135–137).
- **Level 7, Food and Fruit** — the five food groups with
  should/shouldn't health advice (U1, pp.1–16) and the likes
  survey. **Level 7, Animals** — the zoo set of twelve and the
  panda dialogue (U4 L2, pp.49–53); the Farmer Bounta song
  (U3 L2, p.37). **Level 7, The Town** — tourist-site advice
  over four regions (U2 L1, pp.17–21).
- **Level 14, The Paragraph / First, Then, Finally** —
  two-paragraph tasks (pp.20–21, 25) and the process connectors
  firstly/secondly/thirdly/finally taught as the weaving and
  rice lessons' own frame (U3, pp.29–36).
- **Level 15, Reading Whole Texts** — biography texts
  (pp.70–83), the match report (pp.118–119), listening that
  corrects wrong information (p.84), and the torn-up letter to
  re-order (p.126).
- **Levels 16–17, The Essay / The Story** — personal-letter
  writing from two model letters (U10 L1, pp.124–129; also
  p.58), and life-story reading: two-part biography with
  questions (U6).
- **Level 6, Word Kinds** — the pronoun/possessive stock is
  re-used inside the is-or-has 's exercise (U7 L1, p.87);
  adjectives carry Units 2 and 7 (underline-the-adjectives,
  p.24).

Grade 9 now stands in The Passive (named and tabled here for
the first time in Laos, incl. the present perfect passive),
Conditionals (the first conditional as "Present Real
Condition"; no second/third anywhere in Laos), and Going To
Later (the decided/undecided split against will). The Grade 9
teacher guide (144 pp in the library record) is the natural
next read, on the series pattern;
the Laos run then continues at Grade 10.

## Grade 9 (ມ.4) TEACHER GUIDE — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-10-27

**What the book is.** The teacher's guide pairing with *English for
Secondary Schools, Book Four* (the Grade 9 student book, filed
above): "ENGLISH FOR SECONDARY SCHOOLS — TEACHER'S GUIDE" as the
cover carries it, with the pink "4" mascot badge, the full team
credits printed on the cover itself (the same names as the student
book's cover, name for name), Ministry of Education and Sports /
Research Institute for Educational Sciences (RIES), 2013. **No
ISBN printed** — the cover carries none and the whole text layer
was searched: zero "ISBN" hits. Verified copy: **3,690,633 bytes,
144 PDF pages — both exactly the library record's figures** for
the English teacher entry (VERIFIED-STRUCT), downloaded from the
same public mirror the secured copies came from
(`guides/secondary/m4/m4_english.pdf`). PDF metadata: title
"Eng-TG--Cover-M4.psd," author RIES, created Jan 7 2013; RC4
print-protected like the rest of the series. **Content verified
by render, not by filename** (the Grade 7 lesson): PDF p.1 is
this guide's own cover and PDF p.2 its own Introduction — no
scramble at this address. Printed folio = PDF page − 4 (front
matter Roman I–III on PDF pp.2–4; body opens at printed p.1 on
PDF p.5). The body ends at printed p.139 (PDF p.143); PDF p.144
is a blank back page carrying only the diagonal RIES stamp that
watermarks the whole book. The guide's Contents (PDF pp.3–4)
runs U1 p.1 · U2 p.16 · U3 p.27 · U4 p.41 · U5 REVISION p.54 ·
U6 p.61 · U7 p.78 · U8 p.91 · U9 p.106 · U10 p.119 · U11
REVISION p.133 — the same unit skeleton as the student book,
with no back-matter section of its own: no glossary, no
bibliography in the guide.

**Introduction (printed p. I, PDF p.2) — the whole method text.**
Read in full on the page. The guide is "the fourth in a series
of seven teacher's guides," developed to give teaching
techniques that go with the textbooks; it carries "the necessary
steps of teaching with clear instruction," ideas for preparing
teaching aids, "the keys of the lessons, some reading texts for
listening, some songs and games"; the teacher should have the
guide along with the textbook. It admits plainly that it "is
produced under the time pressure that it cannot be probed into
every needed area. Some minor errors may also contain in some
instructions," and invites comments to the Foreign Language
Research and Resource Centre; signed Research Institute for
Educational Sciences. A Lao half of the introduction follows on
the same page (read on the render; the Lao layer extracts as
mojibake under the series' legacy font). **There is no method
chapter beyond this** — and, unlike the primary guides, **no
per-lesson time allocation is printed anywhere in the English
layer, and there is no assessment chapter**: the method lives in
a one-page English objectives overview at each unit's head
(printed pp.1–2, 16, 27, 41, 61, 78, 91, 106, 119, 133) plus
numbered steps under bilingual lesson headings. The steps
themselves are carried mostly in Lao; the keys, listening
scripts, and model answers print in English — this filing rests
on that English layer, with Lao-carried points read on renders
where they matter, as throughout the Laos run.

**What the guide adds, unit by unit** (all page numbers are the
guide's printed pages):

- **Unit 1 (pp.1–15).** Keys for everything. L1: the five-food-
  groups read-and-write key (proteins from fish, milk, beef,
  pork, chicken, shrimp, snail, egg; green and black beans and
  soya milk also named for proteins; carbohydrate from rice,
  flour, sugar, taro, corn, fruit; vegetables and fruits for
  vitamins and minerals); a Listen-and-say key (1 True, 2 False,
  3 False, 4 True, 5 False); the Listen-and-complete cloze keyed
  word by word in order (EAT, BALANCE, FIVE, PROTEINS,
  VEGETABLES, ANIMALS, CARBOHYDRATES, OILS, ANIMALS, FAT,
  VEGETABLES, FRUIT, CONTAIN, FOOD, RIGHT); a second
  Listen-and-say key (p.7) with the corrections printed in full
  (he needs the advice from the doctor; he should eat meat and
  eggs; Somphone shouldn't eat a lot of fat); spelling-contest
  words: proteins, carbohydrates. L2: look-and-write samples ("I
  like spicy food. / I dislike oily and fat food."); a
  read-and-match key; **Jennifer's tastes table printed** (p.10:
  sticky rice ✓, papaya salad ✗, bananas ✓, grilled chicken ✓,
  spicy food ✗, orange ✓); survey-report model lines. The "Food
  I like to eat" chant step (p.11) is procedure only — **the
  chant's words are not reprinted** (they stand in the student
  book, p.10). L3: the unit overview prints the frequency scale
  ALWAYS, USUALLY, OFTEN, SOMETIMES, RARELY, NEVER (p.12); the
  read-and-choose key (1 BRUSHES, 2 CHANGES, 3 GOES, 4 EAT, 5
  drinks, 6 eat); the guide's own grammar line — Present Simple:
  Subject + V1(s) + Object + Time; and Daravone's-teeth answers
  (pp.14–15): 1 No, she hasn't. 2 Every three months. 3 Twice a
  day. 4 Her mother. 5 She likes drinking milk and eating fruit
  and vegetable. 6 Because milk is rich in calcium, iron and
  vitamins.
- **Unit 2 (pp.16–26).** The overview (p.16) prints L3's topic
  as "The best holiday I've ever had." L1: the read-and-write
  True/False key with corrections printed (1 False — Jennifer
  and her family are going on holiday; 3 False — Khonphapheng
  waterfall is the biggest one in South East Asia; 5 False — it
  is in the northern part of Laos); the dialogue model "Where
  should I go in Xiengkhouang? — I think you ought to go to the
  Plain of Jars" (the guide prints "go **to** the Plain of
  Jars"); the read-and-complete key (ought to / should through
  seven gaps). L2: **the Luang Prabang listening cloze printed
  with all twelve gaps filled** (p.22: OLD, CUSTOMS, TEMPLES,
  FAMOUS, PATTERNS, CELEBRATIONS, BOAT, PI MAI LAO, DELICIOUS,
  LUANGPRABANG-as-one-word, CULTURE, HOSPITABLE); the adjective
  key (wonderful, old, beautiful, famous, traditional,
  delicious, hospitable); look-and-say model descriptions of
  the two houses (p.23: traditional Lao house of wood with a
  large veranda and wooden fence; modern house of concrete on
  the ground with glass windows). L3: the listen-and-say key
  (p.25: 1 No, she didn't. 2 They got there by plane. 3 They
  went to the Royal Palace first. 4 Yes, they did. 5 Yes, they
  did. 6 Yes, she did) and the model "Which is the best way to
  get to Thamting? — By boat."
- **Unit 3 (pp.27–40) — the passive unit, fully keyed.** The
  overview (p.27) names the connectors "LOGICAL ORDER: FIRST,
  SECOND, THEN, NEXT, AFTER THAT, …" — a different wording from
  the student lessons' firstly/secondly/thirdly/finally frame;
  both stand as printed. L1: the read-and-answer key (p.31:
  1 It is a Lao traditional handicraft. 2 It helps Lao women to
  gain more income for their families. 3 There are four main
  steps. 4 They cut the fabric into pieces for sinhs, table
  cloths and other uses. 5 The threads are often dyed in
  traditional ways); **the Weaving listening cloze printed WITH
  its answers filled** (p.32: (1) HANDICRAFT, (2) WOMEN,
  (3) INCOME, (4) HOBBY, (5) STEPS, (6) ARE COLLECTED,
  (7) PATTERNS, (8) IS PREPARED, (9) LOOM, (10) TIME,
  (11) MONTH, (12) FABRIC, (13) BE USED — gap 13 filled as two
  words, "be used"); the grammar-note key prints the model "The
  classroom is cleaned every day" beside BE (am/is/are) + past
  participle; the write key (p.33: ARE SENT, ARE WOVEN, ARE
  WORN, IS USED, ARE DESIGNED). L2: the farming look-and-write
  key (p.34: sickle, seeds, sow, pull up the seedling, seedling,
  harvest, harrow, plough, replant, barn); the read-and-say
  True/False key (pp.35–36: 1 True, 2 False, 3 False, 4 False,
  5 True, 6 False, 7 True, 8 True); the picture-ordering key
  prints as picture labels; the write model opens "First, the
  plant is chosen. Second, …" with the step naming logical
  orders/connectors and passive verbs. The Farmer Bounta song
  step (p.36) is procedure only — **no song words**. L3:
  read-and-practise verb pairs (lose/lost, find/found,
  borrow/borrowed, bring/brought, leave/left); the ask-and-
  answer key (p.38: 1 She left them outside at the front door.
  2 They are black. 3 They're made of leather. 4 No, they
  aren't. 5 She bought them in Australia. 6 No, they haven't);
  **the "My shoes have been found" listening dialogue printed
  as a script with its gaps keyed** (p.39: (2) What, (3) BEEN,
  (4) FOUND, (5) my — "She's my brother's girlfriend" —
  (6) DID, (7) LEFT, (8) SHOES, (10) RIGHT, (11) YOU, (12) LOT;
  gap (1), in the opening line "Jenny, I've … a good news for
  you," did not decode cleanly and is not asserted here); the
  write-and-say key (pp.39–40) gives nine model pairs — sample:
  I lost my pencil case yesterday. / It has been found. — 2 My
  sister lost her bicycle last week. / It hasn't been found
  yet. … 8 My friends lost his keys yesterday. / They have
  been found. (the friends/his mismatch is the guide's own, see
  Slips). Against the student book's slip "have been foond"
  (student p.38): the guide's script and keys print "found"
  correctly throughout.
- **Unit 4 (pp.41–53).** The overview (p.41) names L1 "Future
  Tense (will) + Conditional Sentence," L2 "Conditional
  Sentence," L3 "Formal and Informal ways of asking for
  permission." L1: the will/'ll table (p.42); look-and-say
  models ("Where will you go? — I will stay at home. / What will
  you do? — I will watch TV."); the Present Real Condition key
  examples (p.44: 1 If it rains, I will stay at home. 2 I will
  stay home if it rains); the choose-and-write key (1 HAS,
  2 I'LL STAY, 3 IS, 4 GO, 5 HAVE, 6 BECOME, 7 WILL BE,
  8 WILL TELL); ten look-and-write model conditionals
  (pp.45–46), among them "If I don't understand, I'll ask the
  teacher," "If I have a toothache, I'll see the dentist," "If
  I study, I'll pass my exam," "You'll feel better if you do
  more exercises." **The student book's "If you smoke a lot,
  you will stay shorter" (student p.45) is not among the
  guide's printed models** — the guide's model set is its own.
  L2: the look-and-match key prints as letter pairs in the
  shifted layer (not fully legible; not asserted); the
  listen-and-say key (p.49) is the dialogue's four conditional
  sentences (If the panda arrives soon, there won't be enough…

  including "You will fail the exam if you don't study hard";
  **the zoo puzzle key table printed in full** (p.50, verified
  on the page: cage 1 kangaroo / Australia / 3.00 pm; 2 bird /
  Indonesia / 10.30 am; 3 deer / China / midnight; 4 crocodile
  / Laos / 9.00 am; 5 antelope / Myanmar / 6.00 — no am/pm

  letter cloze printed WITH all fifteen answers filled**
  (pp.52–53: (1) family, (2) Somphone's father's car, (3) zoo,

  (p.53: "Is it OK if I go…?" Informal · "Excuse me…" Formal ·

- **Unit 5 REVISION (pp.54–60) — every revision exercise

  bag is made of plastic"; the what's-the-matter key (p.59:
  1 broken leg, 2 temperature, 3 sore throat — "He's got a

  4 headache, 5 feels tired, 6 cough, 7 stomachache, 8 cold,

  printed as a script with gaps filled (p.60, verified on the
  page — are, doing, shopping, does, She's, pretty, later,
  closing "Ah! she is over there. / OK. See you later. /
  Bye."); **the invitation/offer matching keyed by printed
  arrows** (p.60, verified on the page: A orange juice → 3 "No,
  thanks. I'm not thirsty." · B come to my house on Sunday
  afternoon → 1 "Yes, I'd love to. Is it OK if I come at about
  3 o'clock?" · C papayas → 5 "Yes, please. It's my favourite
  fruit." · D picnic this weekend → 2 "Sorry, I'm afraid I
  can't. I have to study for the maths test on Monday." · E
  come for a walk → 4 "That's a good idea. I haven't had any
  exercise today.").
- **Unit 6 (pp.61–77) — the two biographies, fully keyed.**
  Overview (p.61): objectives in English — ask and answer
  about Prince Souphanouvong and Ho Chi Minh; Simple Past
  Tense; yes/no and wh- questions about the past. L1: a roles-

  Minister of Civil Works, Minister of National Defence,
  President of the Neo Lao Issara, President of the Lao PDR);
  the read-and-answer key (p.65: 1 born on July 13, 1909;
  2 his parents were Prince Bounkhong and Princess
  Kham-ouane; 3 he studied civil engineering in France; 4 in
  1940 he joined the Lao Patriotic Movement; 5 he became a
  President of the Neo Lao Issara in 1950 — "a President" as
  printed); the look-and-write key (pp.65–66: went to Hanoi to
  study · graduated as a civil engineer in 1937 · returned to

  and Cambodia · joined the Lao Patriotic Movement in 1940);
  six look-and-say model sentences (p.66). **The Hangman game
  is printed in full** (pp.66–67): the rules in Lao with two
  worked letter grids building the word MINISTER — the series'
  game instructions in print, answering the Introduction's
  promise of "some songs and games" on the games side. L2:
  the "PAST SIMPLE: QUESTIONS" teaching section (p.68); the

  yesterday…); wh- question models (p.70: "What did you do
  last Saturday?" / "Did you go out / stay in last Saturday?" →

  (p.70: 1 Where was he born? — Luang Prabang. 2 Where did he
  study? — Hanoi and Paris. 3 When did he graduate? — 1937.
  4 Where did he work? — Indochina. 5 When did he join the Lao
  Patriotic Movement? — 1940); the read-and-say True/False key
  (p.72, verified on the page: 1 False — he was the President
  of the Lao PDR. 2 True. 3 True. **4 False — "He devoted
  himself to national dependence and progress"** — the guide's
  own correction line drops the "in-" as printed; its cloze on

  page records that the spelling of the Prince's name changed
  in 1956, 1979 and 1991 (read on the render); the ask-and-
  answer key (p.72, verified on the page: 1 he was a President
  of the Lao PDR in 1975. 2 he served as President 1975–1991,
  about 16 years. **3 He retired as the President due to ill
  health.** 4 he continued as an advisor to the Party Central
  Committee. 5 he died on January 9, 1995. 6 he died of a
  heart attack. 7 answered in Lao); the read-and-choose key
  (p.73: (1) was born, (2) was, (3) studied, (4) was elected,
  (5) worked, (6) died); and the word-box cloze printed with
  answers (p.73: (1) MINISTER of Planning in the first
  coalition 1957–1959, (2) PRIME Minister of the second
  coalition 1962, (3) PRESIDENT of the Lao PDR until the Fifth

  cleaner than the student text's "ill physical illness" —
  (5) LEADER, (6) INDEPENDENCE — "devoted all of his wisdom
  and strength to the course of national independence and
  progress," the student's "course" kept — (7) FREEDOM,
  (8) HEART ATTACK). L3 Ho Chi Minh: the past-time-expression
  ordering key (p.75: in 2008 1st, a year ago 2nd, last
  November 3rd, six months ago 4th, a month ago 5th, three
  weeks ago 6th, last weekend 7th, last Monday 8th, three days
  ago 9th, two hours ago 10th); the sentence-order key (1 I
  went to the dentist's last week. 2 He drove to Vietnam two
  years ago. 3 Where did you go last Tuesday night? 4 They
  came to Vientiane Capital last January); the wh- question

  the first sentence printed in full — "Ho Chi Minh was born
  on May 19, 1890 in Kiem Lien, Vietnam," the guide repeating

  (p.76: 1 he was a Vietnamese politician, poet, and
  journalist. 2 he studied in Hue and Saigon. 3 he visited

  3 intelligent — a person who learns and understand well (as

**Settlements of the student filing's open points.** The
torn-up letter's order is keyed: **2, 3, 7, 5, 1, 6, 4**
(p.121). The sports-carnival plan is filled in full (script
and table, pp.108–109). The personality questionnaire's
scoring — left undescribed in the student book — is printed:
points per answer, totals to Type A/B/C (p.89). The
Souphanouvong retirement wording: the guide's answer key
prints "due to ill health" (p.72) and its cloze "due to his
physical illness" (p.73), both cleaner than the student
text's "ill physical illness" (student p.76); the same cloze
keeps "the course of national independence" as the student
book prints it. The teacher's name: the guide keys **Miss
Smith** in the revision dialogue (p.139); Unit 10's Miss
Buavone stands uncorrected in the student book and is not
taken up by the guide. Not settled: "Kiem Lien" and the death
date "September 3, 1969" are repeated by the guide exactly as
the student book prints them (pp.76, 134) — no correction
either way; Nidda's teacher is asked about but never named
(p.121); the student slip "you will stay shorter" appears in
none of the guide's model conditionals (pp.45–46).

**Honest negatives.** The Introduction promises "some songs
and games"; the games are delivered (Hangman in full,
pp.66–67; the spelling contest's rules and worked script,
pp.104–105 and again pp.117–118; the solved word searches),
but **no song words are printed anywhere in the guide** — the
Unit 1 chant and the Unit 3 Farmer Bounta song get teaching
procedure only; their words stand in the student book alone.
No assessment chapter, no printed lesson timings, no glossary
or bibliography of its own. Two keys could not be read whole
from the text layer: the Unit 3 Lesson 2 picture-order key
(printed as picture labels) and the Unit 4 Lesson 2
look-and-match key (letter pairs in the shifted font); both
are recorded as printed-in-form rather than transcribed.

**New for the course (guide-printed pages):**

- **Level 18, The Passive** — the Weaving cloze with its
  passive gaps filled gives the lesson the book's own listening
  text keyed end to end (p.32); the Unit 5 rice cloze does the
  same for the rice process (pp.56–57); the error-correction
  key marks two perfect-passive sentences CORRECT as printed,
  a ready-made check set (p.58); the shoes dialogue keys the
  present perfect passive in speech (p.39).
- **Level 18, Conditionals** — ten model conditionals
  (pp.45–46), the panda dialogue's four conditionals in print
  (p.49), a ten-answer "POSSIBLE ANSWERS" set (p.49) and the
  Revision 5 completion key (p.57) stock the lesson's Laos
  side with keyed examples.
- **Level 18, The Present Perfect** — the ever/yet tick key
  (pp.122–123), the four keyed ever/yet dialogues (p.123) and
  the tense labels on Jennifer's letter (p.122) give the
  lesson its deepest Laos key set; the deepest-perfect note's
  words are all worked here with answers.
- **Level 12, Going To Later** — the decided/undecided split
  is no longer the student note alone: two dialogues keyed
  line by line (pp.126–127), a five-sentence choose key and a
  nine-gap trip text (pp.128–129), and the party dialogue's
  going-to/will mix keyed (p.139).
- **Games for the course's stock** — Hangman with worked
  grids (pp.66–67) and the spelling contest with its rules
  and worked IRON/EATEN script (pp.104–105) are the series'
  printed game instructions, citable as printed.
- Question and answer stock: the biographies fully keyed
  (pp.65, 70, 72–73, 76–77), the match report keyed to its
  3–1 score (pp.112–114), the feelings and describing-people
  keys (pp.80–86, 93–95, 138).

**The guide's own slips, recorded as printed:** "My friends
lost his keys yesterday" in the write-and-say key (pp.39–40) ·
the True/False correction "He devoted himself to national
dependence and progress" — "dependence" for independence in
the guide's own correction line (p.72, verified on the page;
the guide's cloze on p.73 prints "independence" whole) ·
"worked on many PROJECTES" in the Unit 6 look-and-write key
(pp.65–66) · "a person who learns and understand well" and "a

personality definitions (p.90) · "Yuso is the another person"

"accordian" for accordion in the instruments key (p.102) ·
"Congratulation!" singular in the carnival dialogue (p.117) ·
"the new Tome Cruise film" in the revision dialogue key
(p.134) · talkative keyed to the opposite "calm" (p.137) ·
"smart" filed under Appearance in the sorting chart (p.138) ·
the Unit 9 overview repeating the student book's
"Congratualtions!" (p.106). The guide's Introduction warns
that minor errors may stand in its instructions; these are
them, filed as printed.

**FULLY READ.** Every page of the guide was read: the cover
and Introduction on renders, the Contents, all eleven units'
overviews, steps, scripts, and keys through the decoded
English layer, Lao-carried points on renders where they
mattered, and the blank stamped back page. The PDF is kept at

## Grade 10 (ມ.5) Student Textbook — FULLY READ + CITATION CHECK PASSED 2026-10-27

The fifth book of the SECONDARY series ("Book Five") — it follows
Book Four (Grade 9, filed above) in the same seven-book run for
the 12-year school system. The Somphone-and-Jennifer story line
closed in Book Four and does not return; Book Five is built from
stand-alone texts and dialogues instead. Source copy: the public
mirror the library's secured copy came from (GitHub
Xaypanya/Lao-Textbook, `textbooks/secondary/m5/m5_english.pdf`).
Working copy verified against the library record before
reading: **5,400,059 bytes, 153 PDF pages — both match the
record exactly (the English student entry)**. **Content
verified by cover, not by filename alone** (the Grade 7 guide
lesson): PDF p.1 rendered is this book's own cover spread —
"ENGLISH 5 FOR LAO SECONDARY SCHOOLS," the spine reading
"ENGLISH FOR LAO SECONDARY SCHOOLS M5," "STUDENT'S BOOK,"
Ministry of Education **and Sports** / Research Institute for
Educational Sciences, **2014** — and PDF p.2 is its own
title-and-credits page, "ENGLISH For Upper Secondary Schools"
over a red **5**. The file is the book its record says it is.
Read page by page from the text layer; the picture-carried
pages that matter (cover spread, credits page, the song page
p.135) were read on renders; the Lao layer extracts as mojibake
under the series' legacy font encoding, and stretches of the
English run in a Caesar-shifted embedded font — both quirks are
the file's, not the print's. Every page carries the diagonal
RIES stamp watermark.

## The book itself

"ENGLISH FOR LAO SECONDARY SCHOOLS" on the cover, "ENGLISH For
Upper Secondary Schools" on the title page — the word **Upper**
enters the title with Book Five, the first of the three upper-
secondary books. The cover badge is a green **"5"** (the title
page's is red), and the record's Grade 10 (ມ.5, secondary year
5) placement is the series position (Book Five of seven).
Unlike Books Three and Four, the credits stand on their own
page (PDF p.2) and are assigned **by unit**: Writers Bouakeo
Vilaylath (units 1, 2), Bounthapany Bounxouay (unit 3),
Viengkham Phonpraseuth (unit 4), Manivone Phaxayavong (units
5, 6), Thongsouk Keomany (units 8, 9) and Khamphanh
Phimsipasom (units 7, 10, 11) — the same team as Books One–
Four, reshuffled; Managing Editors Khamphanh Phimsipasom and
Viengkham Phonpraseuth; Editor in Chief Assoc. Prof. Dr.
Bouasavanh Keovilay; Design and Layout Khonesavanh
Chanthavixay (a new name in the design seat). Imprint year
**2014** (Book One 2010, Book Two 2011, Book Three 2012, Book
Four 2013). The back-cover panel (PDF p.1, read on the render)
carries the print data in Lao: printed by Eastern Printing
Public Co. Ltd., 27.03.2014, run of 17,185 copies, size 18×25
cm. The Introduction (PDF p.3, printed p. i) states the book's
place in the same words as the earlier books: the fifth of
seven textbooks answering the reform of general education from
11 years to 12; the extra secondary year added in academic
year 2009–2010; written by the Foreign Language Resource
Centre at RIES with the Faculty of Letters, National
University of Laos — its English half names the funder as the
**Basic Education Sector Development Project (BESDP)**, while
the Acknowledgements (PDF p.4) thank the **Secondary Education
Sector Development Project (SESDP)** for equipment and money,
and name two outside editors, Ms. Sharon Rose Lin Masa and
Michael Brown, "for their collaboration in editing the
textbook"; the series' standing admission stands ("the
textbook is produced under the time pressure; therefore, some
minor errors could not be avoided"). **No ISBN printed
anywhere** — cover spread and credits checked on renders, and
the whole text layer searched (zero hits for "ISBN").

**Shape.** Contents (PDF pp.5–6, printed pp. iii–v) lists
eleven units, and the body's own running feet number them the
same way: Units 1–5 are content units of three lessons each;
**Unit 6 is the mid-book Revision (Review of Units 1–5,
printed pp.75–80)**; Units 7–10 are content units of three
lessons each; **Unit 11 is the closing Revision (Review of
Units 6–10, printed pp.141–146)** — so 30 content lessons and
2 revision units, then **Bibliographies (printed p.147)**.
Unlike Book Four there is no glossary. The lesson shape is the
series' grown one: an objective line ("In this lesson students
will learn…"), a numbered Vocabulary list with Lao meanings,
Listen and repeat, a dialogue or reading text, then exercises
numbered through the lesson (Read and answer, Read and match,
Listen and complete, Grammar note where one falls, Write).
Printed folio = PDF page − 6 throughout the body (printed p.1
= PDF p.7; printed p.147 = PDF p.153, confirmed on renders);
the front matter carries Roman folios i–v (PDF pp.3–6).

**Unit 1 — Families (printed pp.1–15).**
- **Lesson 1 My family (pp.1–5).** Objective: adjectives of
  quality to describe family members. Vocabulary: actually,
  appearance, eldest, personality, somewhere, bun, fringe,
  puppy. Dialogue (Vilay and Boun): have got for family, "Are
  you the eldest? — No, I'm the second." Reading text "My
  family" (Kham's family: parents Somchit and Khaykham, two
  sisters, two brothers, grandma Oun) written in description
  adjectives — slim, fat, pretty, quiet, shy, fringe, bun —
  then Read and match to the picture, a family tree to
  complete, five written questions. **Grammar note (p.4): the
  two faces of 's — is ("He is tall = He's tall") against has
  ("She has got long hair = She's got long hair")**, with an
  eight-sentence sort exercise and an is/are/has/have
  completion. Opposites of adjectives (quiet–noisy…). **Write
  (p.5): two paragraphs about your family** — paragraph one
  the members and their names, paragraph two their
  appearances and personalities, each with a model opening.
  Word puzzle: 11 family words and 11 adjectives hidden.
- **Lesson 2 Lao traditional families (pp.6–10).** Reading
  text "The Lao traditional family": obligations of mutual
  help and support, work shared by age and gender — men's
  heavy farm work, hunting, building; women's domestic tasks
  (carrying water and firewood, husking rice, cooking,
  spinning and weaving); children brought up self-reliant,
  expected to obey elders. Vocabulary: expect, support,
  hunting, towards, skill, spin, firewood, gender, tradition,
  obligation, according to. Listening cloze (10 gaps): Vannaly,
  24, telling her family's farm life in the first person.
  **Grammar note (p.9): the possessive noun — 's read as the
  "of" paraphrase** ("My husband's name is Khamla = The name
  of my husband is Khamla"), then family-relationship
  completions (daughter-in-law, sister-in-law…). Write (p.10):
  a paragraph about what work you and your family do.
- **Lesson 3 Modern and traditional families (pp.11–15).**
  Dialogue with a tag question in use ("You're having a
  workshop in Xiengkhouang, aren't you?"). Reading text "A
  family in Vientiane" (Somphanh and Chanthalay, married
  twelve years, an apartment, the government-job wife busier
  than the engineer husband, who does most of the chores) —
  the present perfect stands in the text untabled ("have been
  married for twelve years"). **Grammar note (p.13): do the +
  V-ing for chores** — do the shopping / cooking / ironing /
  gardening. Listening cloze (11 gaps): "Modern and
  traditional families," the comparison text (family size
  decreased, women fewer children, more freedom and
  equality). Write: the same comparison filled into a
  Traditional/Modern table (work, education, women, family
  size). Crossword on the unit's words.

**Unit 2 — Homes (printed pp.16–30).**
- **Lesson 1 Lao traditional houses (pp.16–20).** Reading
  text "My house" (Chamnong, 16, of Pakse): a wood house with
  a thatched roof on stilts about two metres high, a veranda
  for eating, receiving guests and relaxing, the mother's
  loom under the house, a well and an outside toilet — with
  the picture's parts labelled (stilts, loom, fence, well…).
  Listening ordering (1–6) over six kitchen utensils, each
  described in the made-of / used-for shape: "The mortar is
  made of stone and the pestle is made of wood. They are used
  for crushing food." Read and translate (English→Lao).
  **Grammar note (p.20): the used-for transformation** —
  "You sit on a stool. → A stool is used for sitting on,"
  five rewrites (chop, keep, crush, write, weave) — the
  passive shape worked as a transformation, never named.
  Write: describe a traditional Lao house you have seen.
- **Lesson 2 Modern houses (pp.21–25).** Reading text
  "Uncle Khamta's house" (brick, tiled roof, glass windows,
  two floors, a garage, a yard covered with concrete).
  **Grammar note (p.23): there's / there are** with a
  six-sentence completion. Read and translate into Lao (four
  sentences). Listen and tick (9 items, an apartment's rooms
  and positions). Ask and answer about your own house. Write:
  describe a modern house you have seen.
- **Lesson 3 Looking for an accommodation (pp.26–30).**
  Dialogue: Susan telephones a landlord about a rented house.
  Reading: three rental advertisements (Pakse, Luang Prabang,
  Vientiane) with a 15-item completion across the three.
  **Grammar note (pp.29–30): the comparative of adjectives**
  — one syllable add -er; two or more syllables use more —
  tabled with examples (large→larger, nice→nicer, modern→more
  modern) and a completion exercise; the lesson opens with a
  comparison chart to speak from ("The house in Pakse is
  bigger than the apartment in Vientiane"). Listen and tick
  (two houses compared). Write: six sentences comparing
  traditional and modern houses.

**Unit 3 — At School (printed pp.31–45).**
- **Lesson 1 School timetable (pp.31–35).** School subjects
  matched to Lao (maths, physics, chemistry, biology, foreign
  language, geography, history, Lao, civics, meetings,
  literature, free period); a timetable listening completion.
  Reading text: Khampheng of Vientiane Secondary School and
  her interests — really likes, enjoys playing, keen on,
  prefers painting to woodcarving, interested in, would like
  to study. **Grammar note (p.34): prepositions at / in / on
  for time** — at eleven o'clock, in the morning / in August
  / in 1997, on Wednesday / on Monday morning — with a
  completion exercise, and a read-and-write where a numbered
  self-introduction text (Kham of DoneNoun Secondary School)
  is matched to ten questions (a–j). Write: five sentences
  about your own school timetable.
- **Lesson 2 School disciplines (pp.36–40).** Reading text:
  the Lao discipline-points system — every student starts the
  year with **600 points**; losing 100 brings the parents in;
  losing 600 means repeating the class; a penalty table runs
  from 10 points (miss an activity) through 20 (no uniform,
  late, miss saluting the flag), 40 (long hair for boys, drop
  rubbish, phone in class), 400 (weapons, fighting), 500
  (guiding friends to bad things) to 600 (drugs, stealing).
  **Grammar note (p.39): the first conditional, named and
  tabled in both orders** — "If + S + present tense…, S + will
  + infinitive…" ("If you don't wear a uniform, you will lose
  10 points") and reversed ("You will lose 100 points if you
  smoke cigarettes"). Write: five rules, two sentences each
  (a must-not sentence + its conditional). Read and complete:
  Vongdeuane spending her aunt's money, six conditional
  completions. Read and write: six sentences with planted
  spelling errors (freind, studys, inteligent, writting,
  beautifull, allways) to rewrite correctly. Game: a
  conditional chain round the class — a mistake puts you out.
- **Lesson 3 School activities (pp.41–45).** **Grammar note
  (p.42): the present continuous tabled whole** (S + am/is/
  are + V-ing, questions with short answers) — revision of
  Book One's tense, worked over schoolyard pictures: eight
  written sentences about a picture, ask-and-answer over
  sports pictures (volleyball, badminton, football, chess,
  basketball, sepak takraw, table tennis, petanque). Reading
  text: Somchanh, 15, Vientiane high school, art class, the
  school football team.

**Unit 4 — Animals (printed pp.45–59).**
- **Lesson 1 Pets (pp.45–49).** Dialogue (Vanh and Sack):
  dogs against cats, comparatives in speech ("more
  intelligent and friendlier than cats"). Reading text "Pets
  in Laos": dogs the most popular, cats useful against mice,
  fish and the aquarium, parrots and mynah birds that copy
  sounds without understanding, the singing canary. True/false
  (8), word-box completion, a pet survey (ask, take notes,
  report to the class from a model report). Write: one
  paragraph of 6–8 sentences about Pheng's favourite pets.
- **Lesson 2 Wild animals (pp.50–54).** (Its objective line
  says "the different kinds of birds" — see slips.) Reading
  text "Birds": thousands of species, foods by species,
  feathers large and small, moulting once a year — with a
  four-column table to fill (food, climates, body, kinds).
  Definition matching (species, insect, wild animals,
  extinct, protected, breed). **Write (p.54): the references
  of the italic words** — They / Some / It / Them / Ones
  traced back to their nouns; the series' first explicit
  reference exercise.
- **Lesson 3 A Lao traditional story (pp.55–59).** The fable
  **The Peacock and the Crow** as a read-and-rearrange (four
  paragraphs A–D to order against the picture sequence), then
  true/false (6). Plot, in summary only: the once-white crow
  paints the peacock beautifully, then, impatient to reach
  the field where the vultures feed, has the paint poured
  over himself — which is why the crow is black today.
  Proverbs matched to Lao meanings (6): Practice makes
  perfect; Every dog has its day; A rotten fish in a pot
  spoils a whole lot; Kill two birds with one stone; He is
  not the only fish in the sea; When the cat is away, the
  mice will play. Vocabulary carries essay and morale (see
  slips).

**Unit 5 — Jobs (printed pp.60–74).**
- **Lesson 1 What's your job? (pp.60–64).** Reading text
  "Jobs": five people waiting at the Employment Service —
  Nongkhan (secretary: can type, can file), Anuxay
  (construction worker), Phanusit (lawyer), Khamsouk (police
  officer), Aliya (waitress) — each job told by its can-do
  list. **Grammar Note (p.63): modal deduction — could /
  might when we are not sure, must / can't when we are
  sure** ("She could be a business person." / "He must be a
  doctor. He can't be a police officer."), a five-item modal
  choice exercise, and ask-and-answer on the same pattern.
  Game: What's my job? — yes/no questions only.
- **Lesson 2 Job interview (pp.65–69).** Interview dialogue
  (Phonenida with principal Khamchanh). Reading text
  "Applying for a job": Phonenida, 30, four years at Mixay
  Primary School, two years before that at the Department of
  Education and Sports, applying to teach at secondary
  school — ten comprehension questions, among them "How long
  has she been working there?" **Grammar note (pp.67–68):
  the past tense and the present perfect tense set against
  each other in full prose** — past simple for the finished
  action, with when; past simple with for for a finished
  period ("I was in Pakse for two weeks," with a timeline);
  present perfect for a past action connected with now, news
  and recent happenings; present perfect with for for the
  period that continues ("I have been in Pakse for two
  weeks," same timeline drawn to now). Exercises: five past-
  simple and five present-perfect verb forms, and a three-
  column verb table (infinitive / past tense / past
  participle: be, come, buy, drink, drive, get, go, eat).
  Ask and answer with How long…? ("How long have you been at
  this school? — I have been here for 5 years.")
- **Lesson 3 Leaving school (pp.70–74).** Dialogue of future
  plans (Vieng, Khamla, Da): good at English and maths,
  interested in IT, might study IT, would like to be an
  author, biologist or librarian. Reading text: Darathip,
  good at maths, physics and English, interested in politics.
  Speak-and-write: a paragraph about what Darathip could do,
  from the dialogue and text together. Write (p.74): about
  what you are good/bad at, interested in, and want to do
  when you leave secondary school (about eleven ruled lines).

**Unit 6 — Revision, Review of Units 1–5 (printed pp.75–80).**
Write a description of a living room from a picture, left to
right (position language modelled). Completions: six first
conditionals; five Perhaps-sentences rewritten with might;
at/in/on (5). Comparatives over pictures and a seven-item
comparative completion; a comparative crossword; five Lao
sentences to translate into English. Hangman with the units'
vocabulary. Write (p.80): **two paragraphs** about what
happens in your home every morning, from a prompt box or your
own ideas.

**Unit 7 — Festivals (printed pp.81–96).**
- **Lesson 1 Lao traditional festivals (pp.81–86).** Reading
  text: the festival year — Boun Pi Mai Lao (New Year), Boun
  Bang Fai (Rocket), Boun Khao Phansa and Boun Ork-Phansa
  (Buddhist Lent in and out), Boun Souang Heua (Boat Racing),
  Boun That Luang, Boun Phavet — most relating to a Buddha
  story; relative clauses run through it untabled ("villages
  that have Buddhist temples," "festivals that people around
  the country celebrate"). Cloze (10 gaps): a Boat Racing
  festival account in the past. Rearrange (8 jumbled
  sentences). Write (p.85): six sentence-openers completed in
  your own words about a festival visit.
- **Lesson 2 Chinese astrology (pp.87–92).** Dialogue in
  which Boua cites this very book ("I read it from English
  for Lao Secondary Schools Book 5"). Reading text: the
  twelve animal years, each animal's people described in a
  full paragraph of character adjectives — rat (busy,
  energetic, charming, generous), ox (hard-working,
  practical, conservative), tiger (brave, confident,
  unpredictable), rabbit, dragon, snake, horse, goat, monkey,
  rooster, dog, pig — with relative clauses throughout
  ("People who were born in the year of the rat are…"), never
  tabled. Cloze portrait of a friend from the character
  words; word-order exercises that rebuild the who-clauses;
  Write: about your own characteristics (three lines).
- **Lesson 3 International festivals (pp.92–96).** Reading
  text: Chinese New Year (offerings on an altar, fireworks
  for the ancestors' spirits, red envelopes, the hoped-for
  first visitor) and Scotland's Hogmanay (the countdown, Auld
  Lang Syne sung in a circle at midnight, the first-footer
  with his coal and mistletoe). Listening cloze (10 gaps):
  Chin Tao's New Year. Rearrange (8). **Sing (p.96): Auld
  Lang Syne printed in full** — two verses as the book gives
  them, the series' first complete printed song since the
  primary books.

**Unit 8 — Communication (printed pp.97–111).**
- **Lesson 1 Making a phone call (pp.97–101).** Dialogue:
  Anna at a hotel reception desk learns to dial (provincial
  access 0, Luang Prabang 71, mobile 20). **Write and read
  (p.99): phone numbers written out in words and read aloud**
  — 021 243578 as "zero two one two four three five seven
  eight," with the spoken forms listed (zero, o, double
  seven). Reading text: how calls are built (country access
  + provincial access + area code + local number; Laos 856)
  with the provincial area-code table, and write-the-questions
  from given answers.
- **Lesson 2 Sending a letter (pp.102–106).** Reading text:
  Somphone at the post office chooses a That Luang postcard
  ("study, study again, study forever") for a friend's
  graduation in Australia and sends it by express mail; a
  price table (express against airmail, Japan/Korea/Thailand/
  China and inside Laos) drives ask-and-answer and a
  true/false-with-correction set.
- **Lesson 3 Greeting cards (pp.107–111).** Reading text:
  everyday cards against seasonal cards, and who they are
  for. Complete a greeting message to a mother from a word
  box (8 gaps). Write (p.111): a greeting message to a
  special person on a special occasion (about eight lines).

**Unit 9 — Markets and shopping (printed pp.112–125).**
- **Lesson 1 Markets in Vientiane (pp.112–116).** Dialogue
  (Vilay and Jose) at the Morning Market. **Grammar note
  (pp.113–114): the superlative of adjectives, with a Usage
  block** — comparison among three or more, the extreme
  degree, "the" before the superlative — **and formation
  rules**: -est on one-syllable adjectives; the most before
  two-or-more-syllable adjectives (with most/least); only
  -st after a final -e. Reading text: the markets — Talat
  Sao (Morning Market) "the most famous and the biggest,"
  Khouadinh for cheaper goods, Thongkhankham the oldest,
  NongDouang and Sangjieng for cheap products from China.
  Adjectives sorted by syllables. Cloze: a Talat Sao text.
  **Write (p.116): a paragraph of 50 words** about a market
  you went shopping to — the series' first word-counted
  writing task.
- **Lesson 2 We love shopping (pp.117–121).** Reading text:
  four shoppers interviewed — Souvannaly of Savannakhet at
  DongDok market, Yamane from Japan (who can bargain in
  Vientiane, something she cannot do in Japan), Somphone the
  student from Phongsaly, and a fourth voice — where and why
  each loves to shop. True/false with correction (7).
  Listening cloze: "Shopping in Vientiane."
- **Lesson 3 Spending behaviour (pp.122–125).** **Language
  structure (p.123): quantifier + of + uncountable noun** —
  a kilo of beef, a can of milk, a loaf of bread, five kilos
  of fish, a bowl of noodle soup — matched and spoken in
  pairs. Reading text: Manivone the price-conscious shopper;
  a shopping table to complete and total in kip.

**Unit 10 — Sports and games (printed pp.126–140).**
- **Lesson 1 The 25th SEA Games (pp.126–130).** Reading
  text: the 2009 Vientiane games — the first time Laos
  hosted, the new stadium on the 13th South Road, the
  elephant mascots Champa and Champi, Lane Xang "the kingdom
  of a million elephants"; the passive runs through it in use
  ("was held," "was built," "were named"), untabled here.
  Cloze (10 gaps) and rearrangement.
- **Lesson 2 My favourite sport (pp.131–135).** Reading
  text: Sone of Xaysettha High School, midfielder, and his
  striker friend Kham. Cloze: forming the year-one team,
  sequenced. **Grammar note (p.134): transition signals** —
  the time-order set named as a set (First, First of all,
  Second, Third, Next, After that, Then, Finally) for
  ordering a paragraph. Listening cloze: the village match
  against Phonthanh, 1–1. **Write (p.135): a paragraph of 50
  words** about your favourite sport. **Sing (p.135): "Are
  you sleeping?"** — the four lines printed, each marked
  (repeat): Are you sleeping? / Brother John / When the bells
  are ringing / Ding Ding Dong (render-checked).
- **Lesson 3 Big match (pp.136–140).** Reading text: a match
  report in the past — Arsenal against Liverpool at the
  Emirates, about 80,000 fans, Torres scoring first,
  Arshavin equalising, Fabregas (printed "Fabricas," see
  slips) winning it 2–1. Listening cloze: a school match won
  3–0. Rearrangement (6). **Write (p.140): a paragraph of 50
  words** about a big football match you have watched or
  played. Word-search puzzle.

**Unit 11 — Revision, Review of Units 6–10 (printed
pp.141–146).** A word/phrase review list to repeat and
translate as a class; three re-read passages with questions —
the phone-call process text, the greeting-cards text, the
festivals text —; word matching in three sets; rearrangement
sets that rebuild the units' sentences, the astrology
who-clauses among them; three cloze completions reusing the
football-team, Chin Tao and Boat Racing texts. Then
**Bibliographies (printed p.147)**, nine entries: Gammidge,
Grammar Works Teacher's Books 1–2 and Student's Book 1 (CUP,
1998); Vale & Heinemann, Picture Grammar for Children 1–2
(Oxford, 1998); Seidl, Grammar One (OUP, 1998); Liz & Soars,
New Headway Beginner (OUP, 2006); Oxenden, Latham-Koenig &
Seligson, New English File Elementary (2006); Bill Biss &
Graham, Side by Side Activity Workbook 1 (3rd ed., 2001) —
every entry dated this time; the "( year )" blank of Books
Three and Four does not recur.

## What's new against Grade 9

- **The present perfect continuous still does not arrive as
  a taught system.** Book Five deepens the perfect instead:
  the fullest past-simple-against-present-perfect contrast
  note in the series (Unit 5 Lesson 2, pp.67–68, with
  timelines). The continuous itself stands in the reading
  texts and questions in use — "She has been working at Mixay
  Primary School for four years" (p.66), "How long has she
  been working there?" (p.67), "How many times has Laos
  hosted the SEA Games?" beside "have been married for twelve
  years" (p.12) — but no note names it, tables it, or drills
  its form. Laos Book Five, like Books Two–Four before it,
  cannot stock Level 18's Present Perfect Continuous lesson;
  the negative now stands across the whole Laos set read so
  far.
- **No second or third conditional.** The first conditional
  is re-tabled in both orders (p.39) and revised (p.75);
  nothing beyond it appears.
- **No reported speech.** Dialogue carries direct speech
  throughout; no said/told note or backshift exercise exists
  in the book.
- What does arrive, named and tabled for the first time in
  the series: **modal deduction** (could/might unsure against
  must/can't sure, p.63); the **superlative with usage and
  formation rules** (pp.113–114 — Book Two's system completed
  with its rules written out); **quantifier + of +
  uncountable noun** (p.123); **transition signals** named as
  a writing tool (p.134); the two faces of **'s** (p.4) and
  the possessive **'s = of** paraphrase (p.9); **do the +
  V-ing** for chores (p.13).
- Reading step-ups: long informational texts (the SEA Games,
  pp.127–128; Chinese astrology's twelve animal portraits,
  pp.88–89; the Vientiane markets, p.114); the first explicit
  **reference exercise** (p.54); true/false **with
  correction** of the false ones (pp.120, 124); a
  spelling-correction exercise with planted errors (p.40).
- Writing step-ups: the first **word-counted tasks** — a
  paragraph of **50 words** three times (pp.116, 135, 140);
  a two-paragraph family piece with each paragraph's job
  prescribed (p.5); a greeting message (p.111); sentence-
  opener completions in the writer's own words (p.85).
- Structure: the revision units return (dropped in Book
  Three) and are counted in the book's own numbering — the
  mid-book revision is Unit 6, which is why Festivals is
  Unit 7 here against Book Four's festival material sitting
  in Unit 8's neighbourhood; credits are assigned by unit for
  the first time.

## New for the course (keyed to the built lessons)

- **Level 18, Conditionals** — Laos (already counted from
  Book Four) re-tables the first conditional in both orders
  over the discipline-points text (pp.37–39) and revises it
  (p.75); still no second/third — the limit stands.
- **Level 18, The Present Perfect** — the series' fullest
  past-against-perfect contrast note, with for in both
  tenses and timelines (pp.67–68); the three-column verb
  table (p.69). Present Perfect Continuous: **not stocked**
  (see the negative above).
- **Level 14, First, Then, Finally** — transition signals
  named as a set for ordering a paragraph: First, First of
  all, Second, Third, Next, After that, Then, Finally
  (p.134), with a sequenced cloze to work them in.
- **Level 13, Describing Words in a Sentence** — the
  superlative completed: usage rules and formation (-est /
  the most / -st after -e) in full (pp.113–114), over the
  markets dialogue and text.
- **Level 13, Mine and Yours** — 's in its two faces, is
  against has (p.4), and the possessive 's read as the of
  paraphrase (p.9).
- **Level 12, Present Actions** — the present continuous
  re-tabled whole (p.42) and worked in eight written
  sentences about a picture (p.43).
- **Level 9, Numbers, Days, and Time** — telephone numbers
  written in words and read aloud, zero / o / double seven
  (p.99); area codes and the 856 country code (pp.100–101).
- **Level 7, The Home / The Town** — the two house lessons
  and the rental advertisements, there's/there are (p.23),
  comparatives over real advertisements (pp.27–30); the
  markets of Vientiane with their goods (pp.112–116).
- **Level 6, Nouns** — quantifier + of + uncountable noun:
  a kilo of beef, a loaf of bread, a bowl of noodle soup
  (p.123).
- **Level 5, How Are You** — the character set grown up:
  twelve animals' worth of describing adjectives in the
  astrology text (pp.87–91), written about yourself (p.91).
- **Level 15, Reading Whole Texts** — the long texts above,
  plus the reference exercise (p.54) stocking Words You Don't
  Know's neighbour skill: tracing They / Some / It / Them /
  Ones back to their nouns.
- **Level 16, The Essay / Level 14, The Paragraph** — the
  50-word paragraphs (pp.116, 135, 140) and the prescribed
  two-paragraph split (p.5): the first word counts and the
  first paragraph-job instructions in the Laos set.
